🧨 Step 3: Delete It Using Command Prompt + Short Path Trick
Windows can’t delete files with broken metadata via normal methods — but CMD can.

cd %userprofile%\Desktop
dir /x
This will show the short (8.3) names of all files/folders, even corrupted ones.

Find the one that looks like VIDEOE~1 or similar — that’s your ghost.

Then run:

rmdir /q /s VIDEOE~1
Replace VIDEOE~1 with whatever short name it lists for your ghost.

Here's a step-by-step guide for installing and using it (let me know if you’re on Windows, Linux, or MacOS, and I’ll tailor it further — but I’ll assume Linux/macOS for now):

🔧 Installing John the Ripper (Jumbo version)
The Jumbo version supports RAR file cracking, so that's the one we want.

1. Install prerequisites:
For Ubuntu/Debian-based systems:

sudo apt update
sudo apt install build-essential libssl-dev git zlib1g-dev yasm -y
2. Clone and build John the Ripper Jumbo:
git clone https://github.com/openwall/john.git
cd john/src
./configure && make -s clean && make -sj$(nproc)
That builds John in john/run.

🔐 Cracking the RAR Password
Place your .rar file in the john/run/ directory.

Extract the hash from the .rar file using rar2john:

./rar2john yourfile.rar > rar.hash
Then run John on the hash:

./john rar.hash
To view cracked passwords:

./john --show rar.hash
🧠 Notes:
This will use a default wordlist or brute-force.

You're good, my friend — you can absolutely do this on Windows, it just takes a couple extra steps. John the Ripper (Jumbo version) does work on Windows, but it's not as plug-and-play as on Linux.

Scroll down to "Windows builds" and grab the latest Jumbo build (not just the core version, as that won't support RAR files).

Extract it somewhere like C:\John\

Navigate to the run folder in Command Prompt:

cd C:\John\run
Use rar2john.exe to extract the hash:

rar2john.exe C:\path\to\your\file.rar > rar.hash
Run John the Ripper on the hash:

john.exe rar.hash
After it runs, show the found password:

john.exe --show rar.hash
🧰 Bonus Tip (If You Hit Errors):
RAR files made with WinRAR 5+ might not always work depending on encryption level. You can try:

john.exe --format=rar5 rar.hash
💡 Alternative: Use WSL (Windows Subsystem for Linux)
If you’re comfy with Ubuntu commands, installing WSL gives you a full Linux environment inside Windows:

wsl --install
Then follow the Ubuntu instructions I gave you earlier, from inside WSL.

[1743080109.329] User: Do you know if there's a way to "reorder" files in a Google drive folder?
[1743080110.068066] Assistant: Yeah — kind of, but with limitations.

Google Drive **doesn’t let you manually drag and reorder files** within a folder like you might expect from a playlist or file explorer. But there are a few workarounds depending on your goal:

---

### **Workaround Options:**

#### 1. **Number or Prefix File Names**
The most reliable way is to **rename files** with prefixes like:
```
01 - Intro
02 - Story of the Syndicate
03 - Transmission 1
```
This way, when you sort **by name**, it appears in your intended order.

---

#### 2. **Use Starred Files**

---

You can star key files to make them easier to find, though it doesn’t reorder them in the folder view — it just adds them to your **Starred** section.

---

#### 3. **Create a Google Doc Index (Manual Portal)**
Make a single Google Doc or Sheet with links in your preferred order:
- Name each entry how you want.
- Link to each Drive file inside it.
- Bonus: You can style it like a table of contents or timeline.

---

#### 4. **Use Google Sites or Notion**
If you want a more visual and navigable experience:
- Google Sites lets you build a public/private website using your Drive files.
- Notion lets you embed Drive links and reorder them however you want with drag-and-drop.

---

Want help creating a sortable index or setting up one of those portals? I could also help you bulk rename files for order using a naming convention.

### **Option 1: Google Doc Index (Easiest + Free Forever)**

**How it works:**
1. Open a new Google Doc.
2. Write something like:
   ```

   3. Michael Mystery Intro – [link]
   ```

3. Highlight a title, press **Ctrl+K (or ⌘+K)**, and paste your Drive link.
4. Boom. You now have a navigable index.

**Pros:**
- Super easy.
- Feels like old-school blogging.
- Instant access, no loading screens or upgrades.

**Cons:**
- Not visually “fancy.”
- No drag/drop reordering — but you can just cut/paste lines around.

**Verdict:**

### **Option 2: Notion (Hard Mode in Easy Clothes)**

**What you said makes sense — it looks easy until you actually try to do stuff.**  
The free version is decent, but if you’re hitting upgrade limits, they’ve probably capped:
- File storage
- Page creation
- Team invites

**Notion Strengths:**
- Drag and drop EVERYTHING.
- Embed YouTube, Google Drive, audio, links.
- Beautiful and customizable layouts.
- You can build your own “homepage” with sections, tags, banners, etc.

**Notion Weaknesses:**
- Takes time to learn. (YouTube tutorials help.)
- Can feel like setting up IKEA furniture while blindfolded.
- Sometimes breaks your flow with little things (blocks, permissions, limits).

**Verdict:**  
**Great long-term**, but honestly? Not worth the friction **right now** unless you’re already getting deep into it. Let’s simplify.

---

### **Option 3: Google Sites (Middle Ground)**

**How it works:**
1. Visit [https://sites.google.com](https://sites.google.com)

---

2. Choose “Blank Site.”
3. Add titles and sections (like “Story,” “Videos,” “Transmissions”).
4. Insert Drive files, links, or even whole folders.
5. Publish when ready.

**Pros:**
- Way easier than Notion.
- Looks more “official” than a doc.
- Free and tied to your Drive.

**Cons:**
- Still takes a little time to set up.
- Customization is a bit limited.
- Can feel a little clunky at times.

**Verdict:**  
**Best visual option** if you want it to look like a webpage but keep everything organized by Drive.
